The polynomial P

This section contains details about\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} P(u)&:=(1+u)\Bigg [\Big (\sum _{j=0}^{N-2}\Big (H_{N,a,b} + \frac{\min (\frac{j+1}{a},\frac{N-j-1}{b})}{(j+1)(N-j-1)}\Big ) u^j\Big )\Big (\sum _{j=1}^{N-1} j u^{j-1}\Big ) \\ {}&\qquad \qquad -\Big (\sum _{j=1}^{N-2}\Big (H_{N,a,b} + \frac{\min (\frac{j+1}{a},\frac{N-j-1}{b})}{(j+1)(N-j-1)}\Big ) j u^{j-1}\Big )\Big (\sum _{j=0}^{N-1}u^j\Big )\Bigg ] \\ {}&\qquad \qquad -\Big (\sum _{j=0}^{N-2}\Big (H_{N,a,b} + \frac{\min (\frac{j+1}{a},\frac{N-j-1}{b})}{(j+1)(N-j-1)}\Big ) u^j\Big )\Big (\sum _{j=0}^{N-1}u^j\Big ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}P(u):=(1+u)[(∑j=0N-2(HN,a,b+min(j+1a,N-j-1b)(j+1)(N-j-1))uj)(∑j=1N-1juj-1)-(∑j=1N-2(HN,a,b+min(j+1a,N-j-1b)(j+1)(N-j-1))juj-1)(∑j=0N-1uj)]-(∑j=0N-2(HN,a,b+min(j+1a,N-j-1b)(j+1)(N-j-1))uj)(∑j=0N-1uj).The following proposition studies the properties of P.

Proposition 2

Let P(u) be the polynomial defined in (29). Then it is a polynomial of degree \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2N-4$$\end{document}2N-4,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} P(u)= \sum _{k=0}^{2N-4} p_k u^k, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}P(u)=∑k=02N-4pkuk,where the leading coefficient \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{2N-4}$$\end{document}p2N-4 is positive. When \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a=b$$\end{document}a=b, the coefficients of P are anti-symmetric, that is we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} p_k=-p_{2N-4-k}<0 \quad \text {for}\quad k=0,\ldots , N-3, \quad \text {and}\quad p_{N-2}=0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}pk=-p2N-4-k<0fork=0,…,N-3,andpN-2=0.As a consequence, when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a=b$$\end{document}a=b, P has exactly one positive root, which is equal to 1.

The proof of this proposition is lengthy and delicate. The case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a=b$$\end{document}a=b is special since it gives rise to many useful symmetric properties and nice cancellations. To focus on the main points here, we postpone the proof to the Appendix, see Sect. 6.4.

The derivative of F

In this section, we study the derivative of the function F defined in (31). The analysis of this section will play an important role in the study of the phase transition of the cost function in the next section.

We have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} F'(u)=\frac{u Q'(u)P(u)-Q(u)(P(u)+uP'(u))}{u^2P(u)^2}-\frac{1}{u}=:\frac{M(u)}{u^2P(u)^2}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}F′(u)=uQ′(u)P(u)-Q(u)(P(u)+uP′(u))u2P(u)2-1u=:M(u)u2P(u)2,where13\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} M(u):=u Q'(u)P(u)-Q(u)(P(u)+uP'(u))-uP(u)^2. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}M(u):=uQ′(u)P(u)-Q(u)(P(u)+uP′(u))-uP(u)2.The sign of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F'(u)$$\end{document}F′(u) (thus, the monotonicity of F) is the same as that of the polynomial M. The next proposition presents some properties of M.

Proposition 3

The following statements hold M is a polynomial of degree \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$4N-6$$\end{document}4N-6, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} M(u)=\sum _{i=0}^{4N-6} m_i u^{i}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}M(u)=∑i=04N-6miui, where the leading coefficient is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} m_{4N-6}=a_{N-2}a_{N-3}>0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}m4N-6=aN-2aN-3>0.When \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a=b$$\end{document}a=b, the coefficients of M are symmetric, that is for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i=0,\ldots , 4N-6$$\end{document}i=0,…,4N-6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} m_{i}=m_{4N-6-i}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}mi=m4N-6-i.M has at least two positive roots, one is less than 1 and the other is bigger than 1. For sufficiently small N, namely \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N\le N_0$$\end{document}N≤N0, M has exactly two positive roots, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_1$$\end{document}u1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_2$$\end{document}u2, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_1<1<u_2$$\end{document}u1<1<u2. As a consequence, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1<u<u_2$$\end{document}1<u<u2, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F'(u)<0$$\end{document}F′(u)<0, thus F is decreasing. While for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_2<u$$\end{document}u2<u, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F'(u)>0$$\end{document}F′(u)>0, thus F is increasing.

M is a polynomial of degree \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$4N-6$$\end{document}4N-6, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} M(u)=\sum _{i=0}^{4N-6} m_i u^{i}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}M(u)=∑i=04N-6miui, where the leading coefficient is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} m_{4N-6}=a_{N-2}a_{N-3}>0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}m4N-6=aN-2aN-3>0.

M is a polynomial of degree \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$4N-6$$\end{document}4N-6, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} M(u)=\sum _{i=0}^{4N-6} m_i u^{i}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}M(u)=∑i=04N-6miui, where the leading coefficient is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} m_{4N-6}=a_{N-2}a_{N-3}>0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}m4N-6=aN-2aN-3>0.

When \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a=b$$\end{document}a=b, the coefficients of M are symmetric, that is for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i=0,\ldots , 4N-6$$\end{document}i=0,…,4N-6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} m_{i}=m_{4N-6-i}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}mi=m4N-6-i.

M has at least two positive roots, one is less than 1 and the other is bigger than 1. For sufficiently small N, namely \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N\le N_0$$\end{document}N≤N0, M has exactly two positive roots, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_1$$\end{document}u1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_2$$\end{document}u2, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_1<1<u_2$$\end{document}u1<1<u2. M has at least two positive roots, one is less than 1 and the other is bigger than 1. For sufficiently small N, namely \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N\le N_0$$\end{document}N≤N0, M has exactly two positive roots, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_1$$\end{document}u1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_2$$\end{document}u2, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_1<1<u_2$$\end{document}u1<1<u2. The proof of this proposition is presented in Sect. 6.5. We conjecture that the sequence of M has exactly two changes of signs, and thus M has exactly two positive roots.

The phase transition and the minimisation problem

In this section, we study the phase transition problem, which describes the change in the behaviour of the cost function when varying the strength of selection \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta $$\end{document}β, and the optimal incentive problem, thus proving Theorem 2. \begin{document}$${\bar{u}}_1< u< {\bar{u}}_2$$\end{document}u¯1<u<u¯2 while \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F(u)+\beta \delta \ge 0$$\end{document}F(u)+βδ≥0 when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u\in (1,{\bar{u}}_1]\cup [{\bar{u}}_2,+\infty )$$\end{document}u∈(1,u¯1]∪[u¯2,+∞)

Hence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_{mix}'(\theta )<0$$\end{document}Emix′(θ)<0 when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\bar{u}}_1< u< {\bar{u}}_2$$\end{document}u¯1<u<u¯2 while \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E'_{mix}(\theta )\ge 0$$\end{document}Emix′(θ)≥0 when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u\in (1,{\bar{u}}_1]\cup [{\bar{u}}_2,+\infty )$$\end{document}u∈(1,u¯1]∪[u¯2,+∞). Thus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_{mix}(\theta )$$\end{document}Emix(θ) is increasing in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(1,{\bar{u}}_1)$$\end{document}(1,u¯1), decreasing in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\bar{u}}_1,{\bar{u}}_2)$$\end{document}(u¯1,u¯2), and increasing again in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\bar{u}}_2,+\infty )$$\end{document}(u¯2,+∞). In term of the variable \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta $$\end{document}θ, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_{mix}(\theta )$$\end{document}Emix(θ) is increasing in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(-\delta ,\theta _1)$$\end{document}(-δ,θ1), decreasing in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\theta _1,\theta _2)$$\end{document}(θ1,θ2), and increasing again in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\theta _2,+\infty )$$\end{document}(θ2,+∞), where\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \theta _1:=\frac{\log (u_1)}{\beta } - \delta ,\quad \theta _2:=\frac{\log (u_2)}{\beta }- \delta . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}θ1:=log(u1)β-δ,θ2:=log(u2)β-δ.As a consequence, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N\le N_0$$\end{document}N≤N0,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \min _{\theta \ge \theta _0} E_{mix}(\theta )=\min \{E_{mix}(\theta _0), E_{mix}(\theta _2)\}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}minθ≥θ0Emix(θ)=min{Emix(θ0),Emix(θ2)}.This completes the proof of this theorem. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Asymptotic behaviour of the expected cost function

In this section, we study the asymptotic behaviour (neutral drift, strong selection, and infinite population limits) of the cost function, proving the main results in Theorem 1. To this end, we will first need some auxiliary technical lemmas.

Some auxiliary lemmas

The first auxiliary lemma is an elementary inequality which will be used to estimate the cost function. Its proof can be found in Duong and Han (2021).

Lemma 3

For all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in {\mathbb {R}}$$\end{document}x∈R, we have14\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} 0\le \frac{e^x+\ldots +e^{(N-2)x}}{1+e^x+\ldots +e^{(N-1)x}}\le \frac{N-2}{N}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}0≤ex+…+e(N-2)x1+ex+…+e(N-1)x≤N-2N.

In the next lemma, we provide lower and upper bounds for the number \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{N,a,b}$$\end{document}HN,a,b defined in (8). As mentioned in the introduction, this number plays a similar role as the harmonic number \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_n$$\end{document}Hn in Duong and Han (2021). However, unlike the harmonic number, we will show that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{N,a,b}$$\end{document}HN,a,b is always bounded and has a finite limit as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N\rightarrow +\infty $$\end{document}N→+∞.

Lemma 4

It holds that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{2(\ln {2}+\frac{1}{2N-1}-\frac{1}{N+1})}{\max (a,b)}\le H_{N,a,b}\le \frac{2(\ln {2}+\frac{1}{2N+1}-\frac{1}{N-1})}{\min (a,b)}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}2(ln2+12N-1-1N+1)max(a,b)≤HN,a,b≤2(ln2+12N+1-1N-1)min(a,b).

The proof of this lemma is given in Sect. 6.6.

The following proposition characterises the asymptotic limit of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{N,a,b}$$\end{document}HN,a,b as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N\rightarrow +\infty $$\end{document}N→+∞, which will be used later to obtain asymptotic limits of the cost function.

Proposition 4

It holds that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim \limits _{N\rightarrow +\infty } H_{N,a,b}=H_{a,b}\quad \text {,where}\quad H_{a,b}=\frac{1}{a}\ln \Big (\frac{a+b}{b}\Big )+\frac{1}{b}\ln \Big (\frac{a+b}{a}\Big ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limN→+∞HN,a,b=Ha,b,whereHa,b=1aln(a+bb)+1bln(a+ba).

Proof

We recall that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{N,a,b}=\sum \limits _{j=1}^{N-1}\frac{1}{j(N-j)}\min (\frac{j}{a},\frac{N-j}{b})$$\end{document}HN,a,b=∑j=1N-11j(N-j)min(ja,N-jb). As in the proof of the above lemma, we will link \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{N,a,b}$$\end{document}HN,a,b to the harmonic number \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_N$$\end{document}HN by splitting the sum at an appropriate point, which allows us to determine the minimum between j/a and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(N-j)/a$$\end{document}(N-j)/a explicitly, given by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} N_{a,b}=\left\lfloor \frac{N}{\frac{b}{a}+1} \right\rfloor . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Na,b=Nba+1.We have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H_{N,a,b}= \sum \limits _{j=1}^{N_{a,b}}\frac{\frac{j}{a}}{j(N-j)} + \sum \limits _{j=N_{a,b}+1}^{N-1}\frac{\frac{N-j}{b}}{j(N-j)} = \frac{1}{a}\sum \limits _{j=1}^{N_{a,b}}\frac{1}{N-j} + \frac{1}{b}\sum \limits _{j=N_{a,b}}^{N-1}\frac{1}{j}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}HN,a,b=∑j=1Na,bjaj(N-j)+∑j=Na,b+1N-1N-jbj(N-j)=1a∑j=1Na,b1N-j+1b∑j=Na,bN-11j.Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\hat{j}}=N-j$$\end{document}j^=N-j. Thus:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H_{N,a,b}= \frac{1}{a}\sum \limits _{{\hat{j}}=N-N_{a,b}}^{N-1}\frac{1}{{\hat{j}}} + \frac{1}{b}\sum \limits _{j=N_{a,b}+1}^{N-1}\frac{1}{j}= \frac{1}{a}\Big (H_{N}-H_{N-N_{a,b}}\Big ) + \frac{1}{b}\Big (H_{N}-H_{N_{a,b}}\Big ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}HN,a,b=1a∑j^=N-Na,bN-11j^+1b∑j=Na,b+1N-11j=1a(HN-HN-Na,b)+1b(HN-HNa,b).Note that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N-N_{a,b}=N(1-\frac{a}{a+b})=N\frac{b}{a+b}$$\end{document}N-Na,b=N(1-aa+b)=Nba+b and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_{a,b}=N\frac{a}{a+b}.$$\end{document}Na,b=Naa+b. Thus both \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_{a,b}$$\end{document}Na,b and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N-N_{a,b}$$\end{document}N-Na,b go to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$+\infty $$\end{document}+∞ as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N\rightarrow +\infty $$\end{document}N→+∞. Then it follows from the following well-known asymptotic behaviour of the harmonic number (see (52))\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim \limits _{N\rightarrow +\infty }H_N=\ln N+\gamma , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limN→+∞HN=lnN+γ,we obtain the following asymptotic behaviour of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{N,a,b}$$\end{document}HN,a,b\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim \limits _{N\rightarrow +\infty } H_{N,a,b}=\frac{1}{a}\ln \Big (\frac{a+b}{b}\Big )+\frac{1}{b}\ln \Big (\frac{a+b}{a}\Big ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limN→+∞HN,a,b=1aln(a+bb)+1bln(a+ba).This completes the proof of the proposition. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

The following lemma provides lower and upper bounds for the cost function, which show that it grows quadratically with respect to the population size.

Lemma 5

It holds that15\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{N^2\theta }{2}\Big (H_{N,a,b}+m\Big )\le E_{mix}(\theta )\le N(N-1)\theta \Big (H_{N,a,b}+M\Big ), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}N2θ2(HN,a,b+m)≤Emix(θ)≤N(N-1)θ(HN,a,b+M),where16\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} m=\min _{i}\frac{\min (\frac{i+1}{a},\frac{N-i-1}{b})}{(i+1)(N-i-1)},\quad M=\max _{i}\frac{\min (\frac{i+1}{a},\frac{N-i-1}{b})}{(i+1)(N-i-1)}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}m=minimin(i+1a,N-i-1b)(i+1)(N-i-1),M=maximin(i+1a,N-i-1b)(i+1)(N-i-1).

Proof

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{N,a,b}=\sum \limits _{j=1}^{N-1}\frac{1}{j(N-j)}\min (\frac{j}{a},\frac{N-j}{b})$$\end{document}HN,a,b=∑j=1N-11j(N-j)min(ja,N-jb). We have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{(1+e^x)(1+e^x+\ldots +e^{(N-2)x})}{1+e^x+\ldots +e^{(N-1)x}}=1+\frac{e^x+\ldots +e^{(N-2)x}}{1+e^x+\ldots +e^{(N-1)x}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(1+ex)(1+ex+…+e(N-2)x)1+ex+…+e(N-1)x=1+ex+…+e(N-2)x1+ex+…+e(N-1)x.Using Lemma 3, we get\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} 1\le \frac{(1+e^x)(1+e^x+\ldots +e^{(N-2)x})}{1+e^x+\ldots +e^{(N-1)x}}\le \frac{2(N-1)}{N}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}1≤(1+ex)(1+ex+…+e(N-2)x)1+ex+…+e(N-1)x≤2(N-1)N.Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m=\min \limits _{i}\frac{\min (\frac{i+1}{a},\frac{N-i-1}{b})}{(i+1)(N-i-1)}$$\end{document}m=minimin(i+1a,N-i-1b)(i+1)(N-i-1) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M=\max \limits _{i}\frac{\min (\frac{i+1}{a},\frac{N-i-1}{b})}{(i+1)(N-i-1)}$$\end{document}M=maximin(i+1a,N-i-1b)(i+1)(N-i-1). Since\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} m\sum _{j=0}^{N-2}e^{jx}\le \sum _{j=1}^{N-1}\frac{e^{(j-1) x}}{j(N-j)}\min \Big (\frac{j}{a},\frac{N-j}{b}\Big )\le M\sum _{j=0}^{N-2}e^{jx}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}m∑j=0N-2ejx≤∑j=1N-1e(j-1)xj(N-j)min(ja,N-jb)≤M∑j=0N-2ejx.we obtain the following estimates\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} m&\le m\frac{(1+e^x)(1+e^x+\ldots +e^{(N-2)x})}{1+e^x+\ldots +e^{(N-1)x}}\\ {}&\le \frac{(1+e^x)}{1+e^x+\ldots +e^{(N-1)x}}\sum _{j=1}^{N-1}\frac{e^{(j-1)x}}{j(N-j)}\quad \min \Big (\frac{j}{a},\frac{N-j}{b}\Big ) \\ {}&\le (H_{N,a,b}+M)\frac{(1+e^x)(1+e^x+\ldots +e^{(N-2)x})}{1+e^x+\ldots +e^{(N-1)x}}\le \frac{2(N-1)(H_{N,a,b}+M)}{N}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}m≤m(1+ex)(1+ex+…+e(N-2)x)1+ex+…+e(N-1)x≤(1+ex)1+ex+…+e(N-1)x∑j=1N-1e(j-1)xj(N-j)min(ja,N-jb)≤(HN,a,b+M)(1+ex)(1+ex+…+e(N-2)x)1+ex+…+e(N-1)x≤2(N-1)(HN,a,b+M)N.Thus for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta >0$$\end{document}θ>0 we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{N^2\theta }{2}\Big (H_{N,a,b}+m\Big )\le E_{mix}(\theta )\le N(N-1)\theta \Big (H_{N,a,b}+M\Big ), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}N2θ2(HN,a,b+m)≤Emix(θ)≤N(N-1)θ(HN,a,b+M),which completes the proof of the lemma. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

We can further estimate m and M in (16) from below and above respectively in terms of the population size N as follows.

Lemma 6

For m and M defined in (16), it holds that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} m\ge \frac{1}{\max (a,b)(N-1)},\quad M\le \frac{1}{\min (a,b)\lfloor \frac{(N-1)}{2} \rfloor }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}m≥1max(a,b)(N-1),M≤1min(a,b)⌊(N-1)2⌋.As a consequence, we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}{} & {} \frac{N^2\theta }{2}\Big (H_{N,a,b}+\frac{1}{\max (a,b)(N-1)}\Big )\le E_{mix}(\theta )\le N(N-1)\theta \\ {}{} & {} \quad \Big (H_{N,a,b}+\frac{1}{\min (a,b)\lfloor \frac{(N-1)}{2} \rfloor }\Big ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}N2θ2(HN,a,b+1max(a,b)(N-1))≤Emix(θ)≤N(N-1)θ(HN,a,b+1min(a,b)⌊(N-1)2⌋).

The proof of this lemma is given in Sect. 6.7.

Neutral drift limit

In this section, we study the neutral drift limit of the cost function, proving the second part of Theorem 1.

Proposition 5

(neutral drift limit) It holds that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim \limits _{\beta \rightarrow 0}E_{mix}(\theta )=\theta N^2 H_{N,a,b}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limβ→0Emix(θ)=θN2HN,a,b.

It is worth noting that the neutral drift limit of the cost function depends on the population size N, the reward and punishment efficiency ratios a and b through the number \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_{N,a,b}$$\end{document}HN,a,b, but does not depend on the underlying game.

Proof

We recall that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x=\beta (\theta +\delta )$$\end{document}x=β(θ+δ). Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta \rightarrow 0$$\end{document}β→0 implies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\rightarrow 0$$\end{document}x→0, it follows from the formula of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_{mix}(\theta )$$\end{document}Emix(θ) that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim _{\beta \rightarrow 0}E_{mix}(\theta )&=\lim _{\beta \rightarrow 0} \frac{N^2 \theta (1+e^x)}{2(1+e^x+\ldots e^{(N-1)x})}\\ {}&\quad \bigg [\big (1+e^x+\ldots +e^{(N-2)x}\big )\sum _{j=1}^{N-1}\frac{1}{j(N-j)}\min \Big (\frac{j}{a},\frac{N-j}{b}\Big )\\&\quad +\sum _{j=1}^{N-1}\frac{e^{(j-1) x}}{j(N-j)}\min \Big (\frac{j}{a}, \frac{N-j}{b}\Big )\bigg ]\\&=\theta N[(N-1)H_{N,a,b}+H_{N,a,b}]\\&=\theta N^2H_{N,a,b}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limβ→0Emix(θ)=limβ→0N2θ(1+ex)2(1+ex+…e(N-1)x)[(1+ex+…+e(N-2)x)∑j=1N-11j(N-j)min(ja,N-jb)+∑j=1N-1e(j-1)xj(N-j)min(ja,N-jb)]=θN[(N-1)HN,a,b+HN,a,b]=θN2HN,a,b.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Strong selection limits

In this section, we study the strong selection limits of the cost function, proving the third statement of Theorem 1.

Proposition 6

(strong selection limits)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim \limits _{\beta \rightarrow +\infty }E_{mix}(\theta )={\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \frac{N^2\theta }{2}\Big (H_{N,a,b} + \frac{1}{a(N-1)}\Big ), \quad \text {for}\quad \theta <-\delta ,\\ \frac{N A}{2}\Big [2N H_{N,a,b}+\frac{1}{a(N-1)}+\frac{1}{b(N-1)}\\ \quad -\frac{\min (2/a, (N-2)/b)}{2(N-2)}-\frac{\min ((N-1)/a,1/b)}{(N-1)}\Big ], \quad \text {for}\quad \theta =-\delta ,\\ \frac{N^2\theta }{2}\bigg [H_{N,a,b}+\frac{1}{b(N-1)}\bigg ] \quad \text {for}\quad \theta >-\delta . \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limβ→+∞Emix(θ)=N2θ2(HN,a,b+1a(N-1)),forθ<-δ,NA2[2NHN,a,b+1a(N-1)+1b(N-1)-min(2/a,(N-2)/b)2(N-2)-min((N-1)/a,1/b)(N-1)],forθ=-δ,N2θ2[HN,a,b+1b(N-1)]forθ>-δ.

Similarly to the neutral drift limit, the strong selection limit of the cost function depends on the population size N, the reward and punishment efficiency ratios a and b, but does not depend on the underlying game.

Proof

To establish the strong selection limit \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lim \limits _{\beta \rightarrow +\infty } E_{mix}(\theta )$$\end{document}limβ→+∞Emix(θ), we rewrite \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_{mix}(\theta )$$\end{document}Emix(θ) in a more convenient form as follows:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} E_{mix}(\theta )&=\frac{\frac{N^2\theta }{2}(1+e^x)}{\sum \nolimits _{j=1}^{N-1}e^{jx}}\\&\quad \times \left[ \sum \limits _{j=0}^{N-2} e^{jx}H_{N,a,b}+ \sum \limits _{j=0}^{N-2} \frac{e^{{\hat{j}}x}}{({\hat{j}}+1)(N-{\hat{j}}-1)\min \Big (\frac{{\hat{j}}+1}{a}, \frac{N-{\hat{j}}-1}{b}\Big )}\right] \\&=\frac{\frac{N^2\theta }{2}(1+e^x)}{\sum \nolimits _{j=0}^{N-1}e^{jx}}\Bigg [ \sum \limits _{j=0}^{N-2} e^{jx}(H_{N,a,b} + h_j)\Bigg ], \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Emix(θ)=N2θ2(1+ex)∑j=1N-1ejx×∑j=0N-2ejxHN,a,b+∑j=0N-2ej^x(j^+1)(N-j^-1)min(j^+1a,N-j^-1b)=N2θ2(1+ex)∑j=0N-1ejx[∑j=0N-2ejx(HN,a,b+hj)],where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_j=\frac{\min (\frac{j+1}{a}, \frac{N-j-1}{b})}{(j+1)(N-j-1)}$$\end{document}hj=min(j+1a,N-j-1b)(j+1)(N-j-1).

Now, note that:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (1+e^x)\sum \limits _{j=0}^{N-2} e^{jx}(H_{N,a,b} + h_j)&= \sum \limits _{j=0}^{N-2} e^{jx}(H_{N,a,b} + h_j) + \sum \limits _{j=0}^{N-2} e^{(j+1)x}(H_{N,a,b} + h_j) \\ {}&= \sum \limits _{j=0}^{N-1}\eta _j e^{jx}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(1+ex)∑j=0N-2ejx(HN,a,b+hj)=∑j=0N-2ejx(HN,a,b+hj)+∑j=0N-2e(j+1)x(HN,a,b+hj)=∑j=0N-1ηjejx,where\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\eta _0=H_{N,a,b}+h_0=H_{N,a,b}+\frac{\min (\frac{1}{a}, \frac{N-1}{b})}{N-1}=H_{N,a,b}+\frac{1}{a(N-1)},\\&\eta _{N-1}=H_{N,a,b}+h_{N-2}=H_{N,a,b}+\frac{1}{b(N-1)},\\&\eta _j=H_{N,a,b}+h_j+H_{N,a,b}+h_{j-1}=2H_{N,a,b}+h_j+h_{j-1}, \; \text {for}\; 1\le j\le N-2. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}η0=HN,a,b+h0=HN,a,b+min(1a,N-1b)N-1=HN,a,b+1a(N-1),ηN-1=HN,a,b+hN-2=HN,a,b+1b(N-1),ηj=HN,a,b+hj+HN,a,b+hj-1=2HN,a,b+hj+hj-1,for1≤j≤N-2.By putting all of the above together, we obtain that:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} E_{mix}(\theta ) = \frac{N^2\theta }{2\sum \nolimits _{j=0}^{N-1}e^{jx}}\sum \limits _{j=0}^{N-1}\eta _j e^{jx}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Emix(θ)=N2θ2∑j=0N-1ejx∑j=0N-1ηjejx.Recall that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x=\beta (\theta +\delta )$$\end{document}x=β(θ+δ) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta $$\end{document}δ being the difference of the average payoffs between a cooperator and a defector. We have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sum \limits _{j=0}^{N-1} \eta _j&=\eta _0 + \sum _{j=1}^{N-2}\eta _j+\eta _{N-1} \\ {}&=H_{N,a,b}+\frac{1}{a(N-1)}+H_{N,a,b}+\frac{1}{b(N-1)}\\ {}&\quad +\sum _{j=1}^{N-2}\Big (2 H_{N,a,b}+h_j+h_{j-1}\Big ) \\ {}&=2(N-1) H_{N,a,b}+\frac{1}{a(N-1)}+\frac{1}{b(N-1)}\\ {}&\quad +H_{N,a,b}-h_1+H_{N,a,b}-h_{N-2} \\ {}&= 2N H_{N,a,b}+\frac{1}{a(N-1)}+\frac{1}{b(N-1)}\\&\quad -\frac{\min (2/a, (N-2)/b)}{2(N-2)}-\frac{\min ((N-1)/a,1/b)}{(N-1)}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∑j=0N-1ηj=η0+∑j=1N-2ηj+ηN-1=HN,a,b+1a(N-1)+HN,a,b+1b(N-1)+∑j=1N-2(2HN,a,b+hj+hj-1)=2(N-1)HN,a,b+1a(N-1)+1b(N-1)+HN,a,b-h1+HN,a,b-hN-2=2NHN,a,b+1a(N-1)+1b(N-1)-min(2/a,(N-2)/b)2(N-2)-min((N-1)/a,1/b)(N-1). Therefore, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} E_{mix}(-\delta )= & {} -\frac{N \delta }{2}\Big [2N H_{N,a,b}+\frac{1}{a(N-1)}+\frac{1}{b(N-1)}\\{} & {} -\frac{\min (2/a, (N-2)/b)}{2(N-2)}-\frac{\min ((N-1)/a,1/b)}{(N-1)}\Big ]. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Emix(-δ)=-Nδ2[2NHN,a,b+1a(N-1)+1b(N-1)-min(2/a,(N-2)/b)2(N-2)-min((N-1)/a,1/b)(N-1)].

Infinite population limits

In this section, we establish the infinite population limits of the cost function, proving the fourth statement of Theorem 1.

Proposition 7

(infinite population limits) We have17\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim _{N\rightarrow +\infty }\frac{E_{mix}(\theta )}{\frac{N^2\theta }{2}H_{a,b}}={\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} 1+e^{-\beta |\theta -c|} \quad \text {for DG},\\ 1+ e^{-\beta |\theta -c(1-\frac{r}{n})|}\quad \text {for PGG}. \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limN→+∞Emix(θ)N2θ2Ha,b=1+e-β|θ-c|for DG,1+e-β|θ-c(1-rn)|for PGG.

Unlike the neutral drift and strong selection limits, the infinite population limit of the cost function strongly depends on the underlying game. The strength of selection, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta $$\end{document}β, is fixed. See Fig. 5 below for an illustration of these limits.

Proof

Using\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} E_{mix}(\theta )=\frac{\frac{N^2\theta }{2}(1+e^x)}{\sum \nolimits _{j=0}^{N-1}e^{jx}}\Big [ \sum \limits _{j=0}^{N-2} e^{jx}(H_{N,a,b} + h_j)\Big ]\; \text {where}\; h_j=\frac{\min (\frac{j+1}{a}, \frac{N-j-1}{b})}{(j+1)(N-j-1)}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Emix(θ)=N2θ2(1+ex)∑j=0N-1ejx[∑j=0N-2ejx(HN,a,b+hj)]wherehj=min(j+1a,N-j-1b)(j+1)(N-j-1),we can estimate18\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{(1+e^x)(1+\ldots +e^{(N-2)x})}{1+\ldots +e^{(N-1)x}}\Big (\frac{H_{N,a,b}}{H_{a,b}}+\frac{{\underline{h}}}{H_{a,b}}\Big )\le \frac{E_{mix}(\theta )}{\frac{N^2\theta }{2} H_{a,b}}\nonumber \\ \le \frac{(1+e^x)(1+\ldots +e^{(N-2)x})}{1+\ldots +e^{(N-1)x}}\Big (\frac{H_{N,a,b}}{H_{a,b}}+\frac{{\overline{h}}}{H_{a,b}}\Big ), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(1+ex)(1+…+e(N-2)x)1+…+e(N-1)x(HN,a,bHa,b+h_Ha,b)≤Emix(θ)N2θ2Ha,b≤(1+ex)(1+…+e(N-2)x)1+…+e(N-1)x(HN,a,bHa,b+h¯Ha,b),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\underline{h}}=\min h_j$$\end{document}h_=minhj and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\overline{h}}=\max h_j$$\end{document}h¯=maxhj.

Discussion

The use of institutional incentives such as reward and punishment is an effective tool for the promotion of cooperation in social dilemmas, as proven both by theoretical (Hauert et al. 2007; Sasaki et al. 2015; Sigmund et al. 2010; Han 2022; Duong and Han 2021) and experimental results (Ostrom 2005; Van Lange et al. 2014; Jia-Jia et al. 2014). In past works, although mixed incentives were used, the aspect of minimisation of the cost function while ensuring a minimum level of cooperation was overlooked. Moreover, existing works that consider this question usually omit the stochastic effects that drive population dynamics, namely when the strength of selection, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta $$\end{document}β, varies.

In this work, we used a stochastic evolutionary game theoretic approach for finite, well-mixed populations and obtained theoretical results for the optimal cost of mixed incentives that ensure a desired level of cooperation, for a given intensity of selection, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta $$\end{document}β. We show that this cost strongly depends on the value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta $$\end{document}β due to the existence of a phase transition in the cost function for providing mixed incentives. This behaviour is missing in works that consider a deterministic evolutionary approach (Wang et al. 2019). We also characterised asymptotic behaviours of the cost function and showed that mixed incentives are always cost efficient than either using only reward or only punishment. In particular, we successfully obtained an infinite population limit, as well as those for neutral drift and strong selection.

For the mathematical analysis of the mixed incentive cost function to be possible, we made some assumptions. Firstly, in order to derive the analytical formula for the frequency of cooperation, we assumed a small mutation limit (Rockenbach and Milinski 2007; Nowak et al. 2004; Sigmund 2010). Despite the simplified assumption, this small mutation limit approach has wide applicability to scenarios which go well beyond the strict limit of very small mutation rates (Zisis et al. 2015; Hauert et al. 2007; Sigmund et al. 2010; Rand et al. 2013). If we were to relax this assumption, the derivation of a closed form for the frequency of cooperation would be intractable. Secondly, we focused on two important cooperation dilemmas, the Donation Game and the Public Goods Game. Both have in common that the difference in average payoffs between a cooperator and a defector does not depend on the population composition. This special property allowed us to simplify the fundamental matrix of the Markov chain to a tridiagonal form and apply the techniques of matrix analysis to obtain a closed form of its inverse matrix. In games with more complex payoff matrices such as the general prisoners’ dilemma and the collective risk game (Sun et al. 2021), this property no longer holds (e.g. in the former case the payoff difference, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Pi _C(i)-\Pi _D(i)$$\end{document}ΠC(i)-ΠD(i), depends additively on i) and the technique in this paper cannot be directly applied. In these scenarios, we might consider other approaches to approximate the inverse matrix, exploiting its block structure.

More recent works looked at the effect of indirect exclusion on cooperation, having found that the introduction of indirect exclusion could induce the stable coexistence of cooperators and defectors or the dominance of cooperators, successfully promoting cooperation (Liu and Chen 2022). Looking at prior agreement before an interaction takes place also showed that cooperation would be increased. Thus, individuals choose whether to take part in a social dilemma and those who do are rewarded (Ogbo et al. 2022; Han 2022). Our future work will consider cost-efficiency in these different form of institutional incentives, including the problem of providing incentives whenever the frequency or number of cooperators (or defectors) in the population does not exceed a given threshold.

We furthermore aim to investigate the optimisation problems of incentives such as reward, punishment, and exclusion in complex networks. There has been little attention to providing analytical results for cost-efficient incentives in structured populations or in more complex games, so this would also be an interesting research avenue. Finally, since time is most precious, we intent to explore the time that the system needs in order to achieve a desired level of cooperation.
